PREFACE. 


Tuz resources at my disposition in preparing this 
edition were, in tbe first instance, the two Copenhagen 
MSS., nos XXXIII and XXXIV, marked in my notes 
A and B. For а most valuable addition to these aide I 
am indebted to the never failing liberality of Dr. R. Rost, 
to whom in consequence is essentially due whatever merit 
my edition may possess. From his own rich library he 
sent me the two MSS. marked C and M. : 

B is by far the oldest MS. of the Copenhagen col- 
lection, and in fact very ancient. Though little experienced 
in judging of very old Singhalese MSS., if I may venture 
а guess as to its age I should say that it is at least 400 
years old; the Copenhagen SN., the oldest of our dated MSS., 
from the beginning of the 18th century, in comparison 
with it looking quite modern. The character, which is 
lerge and bold but rather negligently written, differs not 
a little from that commonly used. Some idea may be 
formed of it, when I say that et first I read уа for dha, 
và for pā, etc. A final ye is often followed by a stroke 
resembling the Singh. vowel æ, a peculiarity I bave not 
met with anywhere else. Tbe letter n frequently takes в 
cursive form, whicb by precluding the possibility of a 
confusion with t was of service in a few cases, especially 
in pakkhanna, which the Singbalese usually write pakkbanta 


IV 


or confound with pakkanta, But in spite of its age B is 
far from presenting throughout a good text ; its principal fault 
ів the occasional omission of parallel clauses,! and it not 
unfrequently gives absurd readings. On the other hand it 
often preserves the correct reading corrupted in À and C, 
which I presume may be coneidered pretty fair specimens 
of the common run of Singhalese copies. A and B abound 
in corrections, which I have noted Ab, etc.; in C and M, 
being modern copies not much read by native scholars, 
they are unfreguent. 

The various readings of a fourth Singh. MS. (D) 
were communicated to me by the late R. O. Childers, but 
no farther than the end of the Bāhirakatbā (p.24). 

M is в Burmese МВ. and partakes ef the peculiarities 
of its compeers. In the first place, ite spelling ів of course 
Burmese. The orthography used in Birmeh — I am too 
little acquainted with Siamese MSS. to be sure whether the 
remark is applicable to these likewise, but I am disposed to 
think so, generally speaking — ів not much to the taste of 
European Pali scholars, for it abounds in gross blunders 
most puzzling to those familiar with the comparative cor- 
rectness of the better sort of Singh. MSS. It is, however, 
but fair to add that on closer acquaintance certain spellings 
are met with which strike our attention by agreeing closer 
with Sanskrit or etymology than the corresponding Sing- 
halese forms. Now the Burmese can scarcely be suspected 
of introducing Sanskritisms, and it is rather to be pre- 
sumed that in such cases they have been the sole pre- 
servers of the true and original Pali form. “Thus they 
write bhingāra, patikacc' eva, pidhīyati, samiāj”, ete., for Singh. 
bhinkéra, patigacc' eva, pithiyati, samminj?, etc. I suppose that 


t Towards the end there is а larger lacuna extending from 
{taritarena p. 401 !? to taya ca p.416'7. A more recent hand, be- 
ginning at parik!tt. p. 401° and marked В’ in the various readings, 
supplies the rest of the text. 


v 


we shall have to adopt such Burmese readings in editing 
old texts, and I mean to do so in my forthcoming edition 
of the Mejjhimanikāyo. But in the case of a text composed 
in Ceylon, it is doubtful whether we. are justified in doing 
as much, es we are ignorant of the exact age of those 
Singhalese readings. For which reason I have in this text 
throughout retained the latter. 

Becondly, M presenta, not the traditional text of the 
Singh. MSS., but a revised one, like many other Burmese 
copies especially of uncanonical writiuge. The plan was 
to render the text more easily intelligible to readers not 
very familiar with Pali. Hence, if the construction is 
slightly intricate, the words are traneposed, what sbould 
be understood is supplied, for a less familiar word one 
better known is substituted, eto. In some cases the cor- 
rector has done good service by amending errors in the 
text handed down. For I have not noticed a single cage 
of any note where there is good reason for supposing 
ihat the original text handled by the corrector differed 
from that of our Singh. MSS., especially that of B, where 
it disagrees with AC. Some of these amendments are very 
good, and I have adopted them in my text. A considerably 
lerger number of errors were left untouched, and a few 
of them I have tried to do away with on my own account. 

But it cannot be expected that I should have been 
able to make everything smooth; not a few errors l have 
been obliged to leave as they are. The text has not 
reached our day without suffering from the ravages of 
time. The table of contents given at p.2 does not agree 
very well with the present state of the text. There are, 
besides minor corruptions, several lacunas here and there; 
interpolations and perhaps transpositions have been intro- 
duced, and the close of the work has been long since lost. 
A spurious supplement, or rather two, were added, per- 
haps in Siam; at least the Singh. MSS. end with the 


VI 


notice, "Siyamdesato (Sāmindadesato Ab) anitapotthaketo is- 
satthassa panhato patthāya pariyosānavacanāni gahetvā likhitan * ti 
jünitabbam." М in this place has independent and partly 
better readings, manifestly derived from в MS. different 
from the Siamese one in question. B is the only one of 
шу MSS. which marks precisely where the lacuna begins, 
for it ends there with the title “Milindapatham.” І might 
have chosen that form of tbe name for the title of the 
book, but I preferred "Milindepasbo," because, as we learn 
from Rask, Turnour, and others, such is its usual name in 
Ceylon. The supplement has *'Milindapanh&," which, ав 
titles of books are generally collective singulars, and as 
the Burmese and probably also tbe Siamese prefer paūhā 
io patho or panham, is rather a feminine than a plural. 

In point of spelling 1 bave scarcely at all deviated 
from my predecessors. For want of type the guttural nasal 
remained unmarked, but I employ it wherever it is due, 
even if the Singhalese, and often also the Burmese, sub- 
stitute an anusvāra. For vy l have written by throughout, 
like M. Senart;,the Burmese have it so universally, and 
my oldest Singh. MB. mostly То mark sandhi 1 have 
allowed myself the innovation of a "Makkeph," as it is 
called in Hebrew grammar. To my mind it is not quite 
correct to make Pali words end in m, i, d, etc, without 
a hint of the reason, or to write e. g. tai Āeva, as if бета 
were an independent form of yeva. But 1 am far from 
laying any stress on the matter. 

As regards the question of the date at which the 
Milindapsüho was either originally composed or converted 
into its present shape, I regret my inability to be as pre- 
cise as desirable. After the identity of Milinda with the 
Bactriun king Menander has been placed beyond doubt. it 
is evident that the original work cannot beolder than the 
middle of the second century в. c. and from its utter 
want of historical actuality it must be not a little younger, 


VII 


at least a hundred years or two. But it is next to impossible 
to conceive that any tradition about Milinda should have 
reached Ceylon and that the work should have been com- 
posed tbere. It must have been imported from northern 
India, where alone the name of the conqueror can have 
been preserved. In all probability the original was in 
Sanskrit, and our text is a translation. There are, I think, 
& few vestiges from which to infer that such is the case. 
The opening phrase *tamyatha 'nusuyate" is not found in 
any other Pali writing, and it is only in Milindapaiüho 
that quotations, real or pretended, are introduced by ''bha- 
vatihe," Неге а new problem is laid before us, viz., at 
what time the Pali version was written, and there is the 
same difficulty about an exact solution. Our text can 
scarcely be older than the first century A.D., but it may 
be younger. There is however a limit which. cannot be 
„pessed. It is older than the beginning of the fifth century, 
for it is quoted by Buddhaghosa, who besides it mentions 
no writings but those of commentators, and to have acquired 
sufficient authority it cannot then bave been of recent 
production. Perhaps we shall not err greatly by fixing 
its date at between 100 and 200 of our era. From the 
Milindapaüho itself no help is to be obtained, for, as it has 
been pointed out long ago, its chronology is utterly worthless. 

The Burmese MS. adds a title to each question, e. g. 
(p. 73), “Raja Buddhanidassanapanham puccbanto ühe: Bhante 
N,... Buddhanidāssanapaīhā dasami.” І wished to have 
given these titles in an appendix, as they might serve for 
an index; but time pressed, and I was obliged to omit 
tbem. They may one-day appear in a supplement, accom- 
panied among other matter by such illustrations as may be ex- 
tracted from the Singhalese translation, printed in Ceylon in 
1878, which I regret to gay has not yet come into my hands. 

Copenhagen, June, 1880. 

V. TRENCKNER.

14413 See MN.63. The questions left unanswered by 
Buddha, are those mentioned at р. 145. They enter into 
many suttas and constitute the nucleus of several of the 
shorter. They form one of those very old texts which by 
being constantly repeated prove their existence before most 
of the present suttas, ond many of which are no doubt 
genuine. ‘Tam jivam tam sariram’ means, ‘Are life (or 
soul) and body identical; this use of the doubled demon- 
strative is not unfrequent (Childers mistook the meaning, 
v. 8. paūho). The last of these questions, 'hoti tathagato 
param * marana, etc. is of particular interest, as proving the 
important fact that Buddha, so far from teaching anything 
about nirvāņa after physical death, waived the question 
and put bis veto on any discussion of the subject. The 
inconvenient interdiction was in after ages eluded by 
explaining tathügato (undoubtedly == arhat) iu this саве 
to mean 'satto;' an arbitrary interpretution, for which there 
is uo trace of authority. But it set philosophers 
at liberty to dive into speculatious on a matter of vital 


435 


interest; whereas the nonentity of the individual was a 
truism never probably controverted within the pale of 
Buddhism. — 14436 Cf. DN.83 (quoted by Childers at 
patho); AN.IV,v,2. — 14536 Dh.v.129. — 14618 The 
reading pbāsū is very uncertain. — 14734 башка here

emphasis. The Singhalese constantly write so; the Bur 
mese scarcely ever distinguish pp and bb accurately. —
